squares to zero. For convenience, we write
\Delta
when we actually mean
\Delta_{\sigma}
in order to not emphasize on the choice of reference half-density. It is important to mention that this construction needs a suitable regularization in the case where
\mathcal{F}
is infinite-dimensional. See also
[
Šev06
]
for describing the BV Laplacian naturally through a spectral sequence approach of a double complex using the odd symplectic structure.

Theorem 4.1.5

(Batalin–Vilkovisky–Schwarz
[
BV81
,
Sch93
]
)
.

Let
f,g\in\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{F})
be two half-densities on
\mathcal{F}
. Then

(1)

if
f=\Delta g
(
BV exact
), we get that

\int_{\mathcal{L}}f=0,

for any Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{F}
.

(2)

if
\Delta f=0
(
BV closed
), we get that

\frac{{\mathrm{d}}}{{\mathrm{d}}t}\int_{\mathcal{L}_{t}}f=0,

for any smooth family
(\mathcal{L}_{t})
of Lagrangian submanifolds of
\mathcal{F}
.

For the application to quantum field theory, we want to consider
19
19

19

More precisely, we want to consider
f=\exp(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}/\hbar)\rho
, where
\rho
is some
\Delta
-closed reference half-density on
\mathcal{F}
.

f=\exp(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}/\hbar).

The choice of Lagrangian submanifold is in fact equivalent to the choice of gauge-fixing. Thus, the second point of Theorem
4.1.5
tells us that the the gauge-independence condition is encoded in the equation

\Delta\exp(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}/\hbar)=0,

which is equivalent to

(4.1.5)

(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S})-2\mathrm{i}\hbar\Delta\mathcal{S}=0.

Equation (
4.1.5
) is called
Quantum Master Equation (QME)
. Note that the QME reduces to the CME for
\hbar\rightarrow 0
.

Example 4.1.6

(AKSZ theory
[
Ale+97
]
)
.

A natural example of BV theories was formulated by Alexandrov, Kontsevich, Schwarz and Zaboronsky in
[
Ale+97
]
. A
differential graded symplectic manifold
of degree
d
consists of a graded manifold
\mathcal{M}
endowed with an exact symplectic form
\omega={\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha
of degree
d
and a smooth Hamiltonian function
\Theta
on
\mathcal{M}
of degree
d+1
satisfying

\{\Theta,\Theta\}=0,

where
\{\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt\}
is the Poisson bracket induced by
\omega
. Such a triple
(\mathcal{M},\omega={\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha,\Theta)
is sometimes also called a
Hamiltonian manifold
.
A
d
-dimensional
AKSZ sigma model
with target a Hamiltonian manifold
(\mathcal{M},\omega={\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{M}}\alpha,\Theta)
of degree
d-1
is the BV theory, which associates to a
d
-manifold
\Sigma
the BV manifold
(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma},\omega_{\Sigma},\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma})
, where
20
20

20

This is the infinite-dimensional graded manifold adjoint to the Cartesian product (internal morphisms).

(4.1.6)

\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}=\Map(T[1]\Sigma,\mathcal{M}),

the BV symplectic form
\omega_{\Sigma}
is of the form

(4.1.7)

\omega_{\Sigma}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma}\bm{\mu}_{d}\omega_{\mu\nu}\delta\Phi^{\mu}\delta\Phi^{\nu},

and the BV action is given by

(4.1.8)

\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma}\bm{\mu}_{d}\left(\alpha_{\mu}(\Phi){\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\Phi^{\mu}+\Theta(\Phi)\right).

we have denoted by
\Phi\in\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}
the superfields, by
\omega_{\mu\nu}
the components of the symplectic form
\omega
, by
\alpha_{\mu}
the components of
\alpha
and
\Phi^{\mu}
are the components of
\Phi
in local coordinates
(u,\theta)
. Moreover,
\bm{\mu}_{d}:={\mathrm{d}}^{d}u{\mathrm{d}}^{d}\theta
denotes the measure on
T[1]\Sigma
and
\delta
is the de Rham differential on
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}
. Indeed, one can check that
\omega_{\Sigma}
is symplectic of degree
-1
and that
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
is of degree 0 satisfying the CME

(\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma},\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma})=0.

This is obtained by considering the
transgression
map

\mathscr{T}\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{M})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Map(T[1]\Sigma,\mathcal{M})),

given by the following diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(T[1]\Sigma,\mathcal{M})\times T[1]\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Map(T[1]\Sigma,\mathcal{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}

where
\pi_{1}
denotes the projection to the first factor and
\mathrm{ev}
denotes the evaluation map. The transgression map is then defined as
\mathscr{T}:=(\pi_{1})_{*}\mathrm{ev}^{*}
. Note that the map
(\pi_{1})_{*}
denotes fiber integration. The
(-1)
-shifted symplectic form on
\Map(T[1]\Sigma,\mathcal{M})
is then given by
\omega_{\Sigma}=\mathscr{T}(\omega)=(\pi_{1})_{*}\mathrm{ev}^{*}\omega
.
Many theories of interest are in fact of AKSZ-type such as e.g. Chern–Simons theory
[
Wit89
,
AS91
,
AS94
]
, the Poisson sigma model
[
CF00
,
CF01
]
, Witten’s
A
- and
B
-model
[
Wit88a
,
Ale+97
]
and
2
-dimensional Yang–Mills theory
[
IM19
]
.

4.2.
BV algebras

We want to recall some notions on BV algebras as in
[
Get94
]
, and how it is related to the original BV formalism. A
braid algebra

\mathcal{B}r
is a commutative DG algebra endowed with a Lie bracket
[\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt]
of degree
+1
satisfying the Poisson relations

(4.2.1)

[a,bc]=[a,b]c+(-1)^{|a|(|b|-1)}b[a,c],\qquad\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{B}r

An identity element in
\mathcal{B}r
is an element
\bm{1}
of degree 0 such that it is an identity for the product and
[\bm{1},\kern 5.0pt]=0
.
A
BV algebra

\mathcal{BV}
is a commutative DG algebra endowed with an operator
\Delta\colon\mathcal{BV}_{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{BV}_{\bullet+1}
such that
\Delta^{2}=0
and

(4.2.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Delta(abc)&=\Delta(ab)c+(-1)^{|a|}a\Delta(bc)+(-1)^{(|a|-1)|b|}b\Delta(ac)\\
&-\Delta abc-(-1)^{|a|}a\Delta bc-(-1)^{|a|+|b|}ab\Delta c,\qquad\forall a,b,c\in\mathcal{BV}.\end{split}

An identity in
\mathcal{BV}
is an element
\bm{1}
of degree 0 such that it is an identity for the product and
\Delta\bm{1}=0
. One can show that a BV algebra is in fact a special type of a braid algebra. More precisely, a BV algebra is a braid algebra endowed with an operator
\Delta\colon\mathcal{BV}_{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{BV}_{\bullet+1}
such that
\Delta^{2}=0
and such that the bracket and
\Delta
are related by

(4.2.3)

[a,b]=(-1)^{|a|}\Delta(ab)-(-1)^{|a|}\Delta ab-a\Delta b,\qquad\forall a,b\in\mathcal{BV}.

Moreover, in a BV algebra we have

(4.2.4)

\Delta([a,b])=[\Delta a,b]+(-1)^{|a|-1}[a,\Delta b],\qquad\forall a,b\in\mathcal{BV}.

The motivation for such an algebra structure comes exactly from the approach of the BV formalism in quantum field theory. Let
(\mathcal{F},\omega)
be an odd symplectic (super)manifold.
Let
f\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}
, where
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}
denotes the space of functions on
\mathcal{F}
, and consider its Hamiltonian vector field
X_{f}
. One can check that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}
endowed with the BV anti-bracket

(4.2.5)

(f,g):=(-1)^{|f|-1}X_{f}(g)

is a braid algebra. Let
\mu\in\Gamma(\mathrm{Ber}(\mathcal{F}))
be a nowhere-vanishing section of the Berezinian bundle of
\mathcal{F}
. As we have seen before, this represents a density which is characterized by the integration map
\int\colon\Gamma_{0}(\mathcal{F},\mathrm{Ber}(\mathcal{F}))\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, where
\Gamma_{0}
denotes sections with compact support. Hence,
\mu
induces an integration map on functions with compact support

(4.2.6)

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}\ni f\mapstochar\rightarrow\int_{\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{F}}f\mu^{1/2},

for some Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{F}
, where the integral exists.
Then one can define a divergence operator
\Div_{\mu}X
by

(4.2.7)

\int_{\mathcal{F}}(\Div_{\mu}X)f\mu=-\int_{\mathcal{F}}X(f)\mu.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

For a vector field
X
, define
X^{*}:=-X-\Div_{\mu}X
. Then

(4.2.8)

\int_{\mathcal{F}}fX(g)\mu=(-1)^{|f||X|}\int_{\mathcal{F}}X^{*}(f)g\mu.

Moreover,
\Div_{\mu}(fX)=f\Div_{\mu}X-(-1)^{|f||X|}X(f)
and if
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}
is an even function, then

\Div_{\exp(\mathcal{S})\mu}X=\Div_{\mu}X+X(\mathcal{S}).

One can then define the BV Laplacian
\Delta
to be the odd operator on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}
given by

(4.2.9)

\Delta f=\Div_{\mu}X_{f}.

A BV (super)manifold
(\mathcal{F},\omega,\mu)
is then an odd symplectic (super)manifold with Berezinian
\mu
such that
\Delta^{2}=0
.

Proposition 4.2.2

(Getzler
[
Get94
]
)
.

Let
(\mathcal{F},\omega,\mu)
be a BV (super)manifold.

(1)

The algebra
(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}},(\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt),\Delta)
is a BV algebra, where
\Delta
is given as in (
4.2.9
) and
(\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt)
is the odd Poisson bracket coming from the odd symplectic form
\omega
as in (
4.2.5
).

(2)

The Hamiltonian vector field associated to some
f\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}
is given by the formula
X_{f}=-[\Delta,f]+\Delta f
, where
[\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt]
denotes the commutator of operators.

(3)

If
\mathcal{S}\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}}
is the operator associated to the Berezinian
\exp(\mathcal{S})\mu
, then
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}}=\Delta-X_{\mathcal{S}}
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}=X_{\Delta\mathcal{S}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S})}
.

Note that point (3) of Proposition
4.2.2
is exactly the case that we have in quantum field theory. Moreover, if

(4.2.10)

\Delta\mathcal{S}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S})=0,

we get that
\Delta_{\mathcal{S}}^{2}=0
, which ensures a BV algebra structure. Note that here we have set
\mathrm{i}\hbar=1
.

4.3.
BFV formalism

We want to continue with the Hamiltonian approach of the BFV formalism for closed source manifolds.

Definition 4.3.1

(BFV manifold)
.

A
BFV manifold
is a triple
(\mathcal{F}^{\partial},\omega^{\partial},Q^{\partial})
such that
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded supermanifold,
\omega^{\partial}
is an even symplectic form of degree 0 and
Q^{\partial}
is a cohomological (
Q^{2}=0
) and symplectic (
L_{Q}\omega=0
) vector field on
\mathcal{F}
of degree
+1
. Moreover, the Hamiltonian function
\mathcal{S}^{\partial}
of
Q^{\partial}
defined by the equation
\iota_{Q^{\partial}}\omega^{\partial}=\delta\mathcal{S}^{\partial}
, is required to satisfy the CME

(4.3.1)

\{\mathcal{S}^{\partial},\mathcal{S}^{\partial}\}=0,

where
\{\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt\}
denotes the Poisson bracket of degree 0 induced by the even symplectic form
\omega^{\partial}
.

Note that we have denoted by
\delta
the de Rham differential on
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
. Moreover, we can, equivalently, express Equation (
4.3.1
) as
Q^{\partial}\mathcal{S}^{\partial}=0
. Similarly, for a BV manifold
(\mathcal{F},\omega,\mathcal{S})
we can consider the Hamiltonian vector field
Q
associated to
\mathcal{S}
through the equation
\iota_{Q}\omega=\delta\mathcal{S}
(by abuse of notation, we also denote the de Rham differential on
\mathcal{F}
by
\delta
). Hence, we can express Equation (
4.1.1
) as
Q\mathcal{S}=0
.

Definition 4.3.2

(Exact BFV manifold)
.

We call a BFV manifold
(\mathcal{F}^{\partial},\omega^{\partial},Q^{\partial})

exact
, if
\omega^{\partial}
is exact, i.e. there is a 1-form
\alpha
on
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
such that
\omega^{\partial}=\delta\alpha^{\partial}
.

Definition 4.3.3

(BFV theory)
.

The assignment of a manifold
\Sigma
to a BFV manifold

(\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma},\omega^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma},Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma})

is called a
BFV theory
.

Remark 4.3.4
.

Given an AKSZ theory
(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}=\Map(T[1]\Sigma,\mathcal{M}),\omega_{\Sigma},\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma})
as in Example
4.1.6
associated to a manifold with boundary
\Sigma
, we can easily construct its BFV data by restriction to the boundary. The BFV action thus given by

\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}\big|_{\partial\Sigma}.

Similarly we can obtain the BFV space of boundary fields

\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\Map(T[1]\partial\Sigma,\mathcal{M})=\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}\big|_{\partial\Sigma}

and the BFV symplectic form

\omega^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\omega_{\Sigma}\big|_{\partial\Sigma}.

4.4.
BV-BFV formalism

Consider now a source manifold
\Sigma
with boundary
\partial\Sigma
.

Definition 4.4.1

(BV-BFV manifold)
.

A
BV-BFV manifold
over an exact BFV manifold
(\mathcal{F}^{\partial},\omega^{\partial}=\delta\alpha^{\partial},Q^{\partial})
is a quintuple
(\mathcal{F},\omega,\mathcal{S},Q,\pi)
such that
\mathcal{F}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded supermanifold,
\omega
is an odd symplectic form of degree
-1
on
\mathcal{F}
,
\mathcal{S}
is an even functional of degree 0,
Q
is a cohomological, symplectic vector field on
\mathcal{F}
and
\pi\colon\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
a surjective submersion such that

(1)

\delta\pi Q=Q^{\partial}
,

(2)

\iota_{Q}\omega=\delta\mathcal{S}+\pi^{*}\alpha^{\partial}
,

where
\delta\pi
denotes the tangent map of
\pi
. In fact, condition (1) and (2) together imply the
modified Classical Master Equation (mCME)

(4.4.1)

Q\mathcal{S}=\pi^{*}(2\mathcal{S}^{\partial}-\iota_{Q^{\partial}}\alpha^{\partial}).

Definition 4.4.2

(BV-BFV theory)
.

The assignment of a source manifold with boundary
\Sigma
to a BV-BFV manifold
(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma},\omega_{\Sigma},\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma},Q_{\Sigma},\pi_{\Sigma})
over an exact BFV manifold
(\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma},\omega^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma},Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma})
is called a
BV-BFV theory
.

\Sigma
\partial\Sigma
BFV
BV

Figure 4.4.1.
The BV theory is associated to the bulk and the BFV theory to the boundary such that we have a coherent coupling.

4.4.1.
Quantization

For the quantization, one chooses a polarization
21
21

21

This is an involutive
\omega^{\partial}
-Lagrangian subbundle of
T\mathcal{F}^{\partial}
.

\mathcal{P}
on the boundary and assume a symplectic splitting
22
22

22

Actually, this is only needed locally, so one can also allow
\mathcal{F}
to be a BV bundle over
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}
. A fiber bundle
\mathcal{F}
over a base
\mathcal{B}
with odd-symplectic fiber
(\mathcal{Y},\omega)
is called a
BV bundle
if the transition maps of
\mathcal{F}
are given by locally constant fiberwise symplectomorphisms. An even more general setting is given by a
hedgehog fibration
where the fibers of the fiber bundle are given by
hedgehogs

[
CMR17
]
.
of the BV space of fields with respect to it

(4.4.2)

\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}\times\mathcal{Y}.

Here
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}
denotes the leaf space for the polarization
\mathcal{P}
which we assume to be smooth and
\mathcal{Y}
is a complement. Note that we can always split the BV space of fields in such a way but it is important to assume that it is symplectic, i.e. that the BV symplectic form
\omega
is constant on
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}
. In fact, we consider
\omega
to be a weakly nondegenerate
23
23

23

In the finite-dimensional case the BV-BFV formalism is not consistent with the nondegeneracy of
\omega
on the whole space and thus one has to exactly assume nondegeneracy along the fibers.
2-form on
\mathcal{Y}
which extends to the product
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}\times\mathcal{Y}
.
For
BF
-like theories
24
24

24

These are perturbations of
BF
theories.
, which includes AKSZ-type theories, one can think of
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}
to be the fields restricted to the boundary and
\mathcal{Y}
to be the bulk part. Using the fact that the BFV space of boundary fields
(\mathcal{F}^{\partial},\omega^{\partial})
is a symplectic manifold, we consider a
geometric quantization
(see e.g.
[
Woo97
,
BW12
,
Mos20
]
) on the boundary to obtain a vector space
\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}
. In order to take care of regularization, we consider another splitting of the bulk part

(4.4.3)

\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},

where
\mathcal{V}
denotes the space of
residual fields
25
25

25

\mathcal{V}
is often also called the space of
low energy fields
or
zero modes
which we assume to be finite-dimensional. The complement
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
is accordingly often called space of
high energy fields
or
fluctuation fields
. Moreover, let
\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}\times\mathcal{V}
denote the bundle of residual fields over
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}
. Note that we also assume a splitting of the symplectic manifold
(\mathcal{Y},\omega)
into two symplectic manifolds

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}\times\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle=\omega_{\mathcal{V}}+\omega_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}.

Gauge-fixing is then equivalent to the choice of a Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}
of
(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},\omega_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}})
. Define
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathcal{P}}:=\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{Z})=\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}})\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{V})
and the BV Laplacian
\widehat{\Delta}^{\mathcal{P}}:=\mathrm{id}\otimes\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
on
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathcal{P}}
. We have denoted by
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
the BV Laplacian on
\mathcal{V}
.
Note that the space
\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}})
coincides with the vector space
\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}
constructed by geometric quantization on the boundary. Thus we have

\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{V}).

Remark 4.4.3
.

Another important assumption is that for any
\Phi\in\mathcal{Z}
, the restriction of the action to
\{\Phi\}\times\mathcal{L}
has isolated critical points on
\{\Phi\}\times\mathcal{L}
.

The state is then defined through the perturbative expansion into Feynman graphs of the BV integral

(4.4.4)

\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}(\Phi)=\int_{\mathcal{L}}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}(\Phi)/\hbar\right)\mathscr{D}[\Phi]\in\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathcal{P}},\quad\Phi\in\mathcal{Z}.

Remark 4.4.4
.

If one uses a different choice of residual fields
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
such that
\mathcal{V}
fibers over
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}
as a
hedgehog
(see
[
CMR17
]
for the definition of a hedgehog fibration), then the corresponding quantum theories are
BV equivalent
in the sense of
[
CMR17
]
. however, there is a minimal choice for the residual fields for which the assumption in Remark
4.4.3
is satisfied by a good choice of Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}
. For the case of abelian
BF
theory, the minimal space of residual fields is given by the de Rham cohomology of the underlying source manifold.

If
\Delta\mathcal{S}=0
, which we usually want to assume, the QME is replaced by the
modified
QME (mQME)

(4.4.5)

\big(\hbar^{2}\Delta+\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}\big)\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}/\hbar\right)=0,

where, for local coordinates
(b_{i})\in\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}
, we have that

\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}:=\mathcal{S}^{\partial}\left(b_{i},-\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\delta}{\delta b_{i}}\right)

is the standard ordering quantization of
\mathcal{S}^{\partial}
.
If
\mathcal{S}
depends on
\hbar
and (or)
\Delta\mathcal{S}\not=0
, we get the mQME from the assumption of the QME in the bulk by defining
\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\hbar}:=\mathcal{S}^{\partial}+O(\hbar)
via the equation

\pi^{*}\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\hbar}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{S})-\mathrm{i}\hbar\Delta\mathcal{S}

and setting
\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}
to be the standard ordering quantization of
\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\hbar}
.
Note that the operator
\hbar^{2}\Delta+\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}
is of order
+1
.
Moreover, we assume that the operator
\hbar^{2}\Delta+\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}
squares to zero in order to have a well-defined BV cohomology. In the finite-dimensional setting we automatically get that
\widehat{\mathsf{Z}}
solves the mQME:

(4.4.6)

\big(\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}+\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}\big)\widehat{\mathsf{Z}}=0.

In the infinite-dimensional setting, where we have to compute the Feynman graphs instead of an integral, the mQME is only expected to hold and requires a separate checking.

4.5.
Gluing of BV-BFV partition functions

Let
\Sigma
be a
d
-manifold and consider two
d
-manifolds
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}
such that
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\cup_{N}\Sigma_{2}
where
N
is the
(d-1)
-manifold which is identified with the common boundaries
\partial\Sigma_{1}
and
\partial\Sigma_{2}
(see Figure
4.5.1
). Then in general, following Atiyah’s TQFT axioms
[
Ati88
]
, we can define the glued partition function by the pairing

(4.5.1)

\mathsf{Z}_{\Sigma}=\int_{N}\mathsf{Z}_{\Sigma_{1},\partial\Sigma_{1}}\mathsf{Z}_{\Sigma_{2},\partial\Sigma_{2}}.

N
\Sigma_{1}
\Sigma_{2}

Figure 4.5.1.
Gluing of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
along
N
.

We want to see how this is adapted to the BV-BFV partition function.
Let
(\mathcal{Y},\omega)
be a direct product of two odd symplectic manifolds
(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},\omega^{\prime})
and
(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime\prime},\omega^{\prime\prime})
, i.e.
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{Y}^{\prime\prime}
,
\omega=\omega^{\prime}+\omega^{\prime\prime}
. Then the space of half-densities on
\mathcal{Y}
factorizes as

\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{Y})=\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime})\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime\prime}).

If we consider BV integration on the second factor for some Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\prime\prime}
, we can define a pushforward map on half-densities

\int_{\mathcal{L}}\colon\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{Y})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\int_{\mathcal{L}}}\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}).

If we consider the space of fields
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{B}\times\mathcal{Y}
given by a product as in Section
4.4
, and assuming that
\mathcal{Y}
splits into a product of two odd symplectic manifolds
(\mathcal{V},\omega_{\mathcal{V}})
and
(\mathcal{Y}^{\prime},\omega_{\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}})
, we can consider a version of the BV pushforward in terms of families over
\mathcal{B}
by

\int_{\mathcal{L}}\colon\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{F})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\int_{\mathcal{L}}}\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{Z}),

where
\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}\times\mathcal{V}
for some chosen polarization
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}
is a Lagrangian submanifold. In particular, we have the coboundary operator
\hbar^{2}\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}+\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}
on
\mathcal{Z}
, where
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
is the canonical BV Laplacian on
\mathcal{V}
. In fact, the BV-BFV partition function is then defined through the family version of the BV pushforward.

The gluing of BV-BFV partition functions as in (
4.4.4
) is given by

(4.5.2)

\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV}}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\mathcal{L}}\left\langle\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{1},\partial\Sigma_{1}},\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{2},\partial\Sigma_{2}}\right\rangle_{N}\in\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}),

where
\int_{\mathcal{L}}
denotes the
BV pushforward
with respect to the map

\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma_{1}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma_{2}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma},

for some Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}
in the bulk complement of
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
and
\langle\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt\rangle_{N}
is the pairing in
\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}_{N}
.
Note that the product of the space of residual fields
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma_{1}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma_{2}}
is a
hedgehog fibration
as in
[
CMR17
]
. The BV-BFV partition function depends only on residual fields when
\Sigma
is closed. If we assume that the space of residual fields
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
is finite-dimensional, which is the case for many theories of interest including AKSZ theories, we can compute the number valued partition function by integrating out the residual fields
\int_{\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}}\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV}}_{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{C}
.
If the glued manifold
\Sigma
has boundary itself (see Figure
4.5.2
), the BV-BFV partition function depends on the boundary fields
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
. In particular, we get

\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma,\partial\Sigma}=\int_{\mathcal{L}}\left\langle\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{1},\partial\Sigma_{1}},\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{2},\partial\Sigma_{2}}\right\rangle_{N}\in\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}).

\Sigma_{1}
\Sigma_{2}
\partial\Sigma

Figure 4.5.2.
Example of the case when
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\cup\Sigma_{2}
has boundary.

\Sigma_{1}
\Sigma_{2}
\partial\Sigma

Figure 4.5.3.
Closing the manifold
\Sigma
.

4.6.
Example: abelian
BF
theory

Let
\Sigma
be a
d
-manifold with compact boundary
\partial\Sigma
. The BV space of fields is then given by
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}=\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)[1]\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)[d-2]
. Denote a field in
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}
by
\mathbf{X}\oplus\mathbf{Y}
, where
\mathbf{X}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)[1]
and
\mathbf{Y}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)[d-2]
. The BV symplectic form is then given by

\omega_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}\sum_{i}\delta\mathbf{Y}_{i}\delta\mathbf{X}^{i},

the BV action by

\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}\sum_{i}\mathbf{Y}_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{X}^{i},

and the cohomological vector field by

Q_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}\sum_{i}\left({\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{Y}_{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbf{Y}_{i}}+{\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{X}^{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbf{X}^{i}}\right),

where
\delta
denotes the de Rham differential on
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}
and
{\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}
the one on
\Sigma
.
The exact BFV manifold
(\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma},\omega^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\delta\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma},Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma})
assigned to the boundary
\partial\Sigma
is given by the BFV space of fields
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\Omega^{\bullet}(\partial\Sigma)[1]\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(\partial\Sigma)[d-2]
, the primitive 1-form

\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=(-1)^{d}\int_{\partial\Sigma}\sum_{i}\mathds{Y}_{i}\delta\mathds{X}^{i},

the BFV boundary action

\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\int_{\partial\Sigma}\sum_{i}\mathds{Y}_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{X}^{i},

and cohomological vector field

Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\int_{\partial\Sigma}\sum_{i}\left({\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{Y}_{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{Y}_{i}}+{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{X}^{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{X}^{i}}\right).

We have denoted by
\mathds{X}\oplus\mathds{Y}=i_{\Sigma}^{*}(\mathbf{X}\oplus\mathbf{Y})\in\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}
for
\mathbf{X}\oplus\mathbf{Y}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}
, where
i_{\Sigma}\colon\partial\Sigma\hookrightarrow\Sigma
denotes the inclusion. The surjective submersion
\pi_{\Sigma}\colon\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}
is given by restricting to the boundary, i.e.
\pi_{\Sigma}=i_{\Sigma}^{*}
.

Consider the case where
\partial\Sigma
is given by the disjoint union of two compact manifolds
\partial_{1}\Sigma
and
\partial_{2}\Sigma
such that we can consider a splitting
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\times\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}
. Then we can consider a polarization
\mathcal{P}
on
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}
which is given by a direct product of polarizations on each factor. One can choose the convenient
\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{Y}}
-polarization on
\partial_{1}\Sigma
and identify the quotient leaf space with
\mathcal{B}_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\cong\Omega^{\bullet}(\partial_{1}\Sigma)[1]
whose coordinates are given by the
\mathds{X}
fields. On
\partial_{2}\Sigma
one can choose the convenient
\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{X}}
-polarization and similarly as before identify the quotient leaf space with
\mathcal{B}_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\cong\Omega^{\bullet}(\partial_{2}\Sigma)[d-2]
whose coordinates are given by the
\mathds{Y}
fields. The leaf space associated to the boundary polarization is then given by
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}=\mathcal{B}_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\times\mathcal{B}_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}
.

\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{Y}}
\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{X}}
\partial_{1}\Sigma
\partial_{2}\Sigma

\Sigma
with two boundaries

and

. On
\partial_{1}\Sigma
we choose the
\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{Y}}
-polarization (i.e. the
\mathds{X}
-representation) and on
\partial_{2}\Sigma
we choose the
\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{X}}
-polarization (i.e. the
\mathds{Y}
-representation).

The case when we have more than two boundary components is illustrated and explained in Figure
4.6.2
. The case for boundary components with mixed polarization is explained in Figure

. However, we will not consider this case in this paper

]
for the quantization of the relational symplectic groupoid. Another (a bit different) approach was considered for 2-dimensional Yang–Mills theory in
[

]
.
.

Example of a manifold with three boundary components where two components have the

-representation and one component has the

-representation. Note that in this case we have
\partial_{1}\Sigma=\partial_{1}^{(1)}\Sigma\cup\partial_{1}^{(2)}\Sigma
. In general we can have
r_{1}
boundary components in the
\mathds{X}
-representation and
r_{2}
boundary components in the
\mathds{Y}
-representation. In this case we have
\partial_{1}\Sigma=\bigcup_{k=1}^{r_{1}}\partial_{1}^{(k)}\Sigma
and
\partial_{2}\Sigma=\bigcup_{k=1}^{r_{2}}\partial_{2}^{(k)}\Sigma
.

Example of a boundary component with mixed boundary polarization.

In particular, we want to assume for the leaf space
\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
the property that for a suitably chosen local functional
f^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
, the restriction of the adapted BFV 1-form
\alpha^{\partial,\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}:=\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}-\delta f^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
to the fibers of the polarization
\mathcal{P}
vanishes. In this case we can identify the space of boundary states
\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
with
\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma})
by multiplying with
\exp(\mathrm{i}f^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}/\hbar)
. Moreover, we can then modify the BV action
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
to
\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\Sigma}:=\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}+\pi^{*}_{\Sigma}f^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
. In our case, we consider the functional

f^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}=(-1)^{d-1}\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\sum_{i}\mathds{Y}_{i}\mathds{X}^{i},

the adapted BFV 1-form

\alpha^{\partial,\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}=(-1)^{d}\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\sum_{i}\mathds{Y}_{i}\delta\mathds{X}^{i}-\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\sum_{i}\delta\mathds{Y}_{i}\mathds{X}^{i}

and the modified BV action

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}\sum_{i}\mathds{Y}_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{X}+(-1)^{d-1}\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\sum_{i}\mathds{Y}_{i}\mathds{X}^{i}.

Denote by
\widetilde{\mathds{X}}\oplus\widetilde{\mathds{Y}}:=\pi^{*}_{\Sigma}(\mathds{X}\oplus\mathds{Y})
an extension of
\mathds{X}\oplus\mathds{Y}
to
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}
and denote by
\widehat{\mathbf{X}}\oplus\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}
the complement such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathds{X}}+\widehat{\mathbf{X}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\mathds{Y}}+\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}.

Note that we require that
i_{1}^{*}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}=0
and
i_{2}^{*}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}=0
, where
i_{j}\colon\partial_{j}\Sigma\hookrightarrow\Sigma
denotes the inclusion of the corresponding boundary components for
j=1,2
. Let us choose this section of the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
. The modified BV action is then given by

\mathcal{S}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}\sum_{i}\left(\widetilde{\mathds{Y}}_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\widetilde{\mathds{X}}^{i}+\widetilde{\mathds{Y}}_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}+\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\widetilde{\mathds{X}}^{i}+\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\right)\\
+(-1)^{d-1}\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\sum_{i}\left(\mathds{Y}_{i}\widetilde{\mathds{X}}^{i}+\mathds{Y}_{i}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\right).

Consider the last bulk term which we denote by
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma}:=\int_{\Sigma}\sum_{i}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}
. Note that, by the vanishing boundary conditions, we get the critical points defined by the equations
{\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\widehat{\mathbf{X}}={\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}=0
. Moreover, consider the subcomplex of the de Rham complex
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)
given by

\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}j}(\Sigma):=\{\gamma\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)\mid i^{*}_{j}\gamma=0\},

where D stands for
Dirichlet
. The residual fields are then given by

\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}=H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}1}(\Sigma)[1]\oplus H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}2}(\Sigma)[d-2],

which is a finite-dimensional BV manifold. Note that we have a natural identification with relative cohomology
H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}j}(\Sigma)\cong H^{\bullet}(\Sigma,\partial_{j}\Sigma)
for
j=1,2
.
Hence, we can define a canonical BV Laplacian by choosing coordinates
(z^{j},z^{\dagger}_{j})
on
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
. Choosing a basis
([\kappa_{j}])
of
H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}1}(\Sigma)
with representative
\kappa_{j}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}1}(\Sigma)
and the corresponding dual basis
([\kappa^{j}])
of
H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}2}(\Sigma)
with representative
\kappa^{j}\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}2}(\Sigma)
satisfying the relation
\int_{\Sigma}\kappa^{i}\kappa_{j}=\delta^{i}_{j}
, we can write the residual fields as

(4.6.1)

\mathsf{x}=\sum_{j}z^{j}\kappa_{j},\qquad\mathsf{y}=\sum_{j}z^{\dagger}_{j}\kappa^{j}.

The BV Laplacian on
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
is given by

\Delta_{\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}}=\sum_{j}(-1)^{(d-1)\gh(z^{j})+1}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{i}\partial z^{\dagger}_{j}}

and the BV symplectic form on
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
is given by

\omega_{\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}}=\sum_{j}(-1)^{(d-1)\gh(z^{j})+1}\delta z^{\dagger}_{j}\delta z^{j}.

Note that
\gh(z^{j})=1-\gh(\kappa_{j})
and
\gh(z^{\dagger}_{j})=\gh(\kappa_{j})-2
. Consider another splitting of the fields

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{x}+\mathscr{X},

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{Y}}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{y}+\mathscr{Y},

where
\mathscr{X}\oplus\mathscr{Y}
denotes the corresponding complement of the field
\mathsf{x}\oplus\mathsf{y}\in\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
as elements of a symplectic complement
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{\Sigma}
of
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
. Note that
i_{1}^{*}\mathscr{X}=i_{2}^{*}\mathscr{Y}=0
. Using this, we can see that
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}\mathscr{Y}{\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\mathscr{X}
which can be regarded as a quadratic function on
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}1}(\Sigma)[1]\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}2}(\Sigma)[d-2]
and has critical points given by closed forms. One can now choose a Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}
of
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{\Sigma}
where
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma}
has isolated critical points at the origin, which is to say that the de Rham differential
{\mathrm{d}}
has trivial kernel. This can be done by Hodge theory for manifolds with boundary. Consider the Hodge star operator
*
for a chosen metric on
\Sigma
with product structure near the boundary, i.e. there is a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon\partial\Sigma\supset U\rightarrow\partial\Sigma\times[0,\varepsilon)
for some
\varepsilon>0
such that the restriction of
\varphi
to
\partial\Sigma
is the identity on
\partial\Sigma
and the metric on
\Sigma
restricted to the neighborhood
U
has the form
\varphi^{*}(g_{\partial\Sigma}+{\mathrm{d}}t^{2})
, where
g_{\partial\Sigma}
denotes the metric on the boundary and
t\in[0,\varepsilon)
the coordinate on
\partial\Sigma\times[0,\varepsilon)
. One can define the Lagrangian submanifold

\mathcal{L}=({\mathrm{d}}^{*}\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{\mathrm{N}2}(\Sigma)\cap\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}1}(\Sigma))[1]\oplus({\mathrm{d}}^{*}\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{\mathrm{N}1}(\Sigma)\cap\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}2}(\Sigma))[d-2],

where
{\mathrm{d}}^{*}:=*{\mathrm{d}}*
and

\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{N}j}(\Sigma):=\{\gamma\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)\mid i^{*}_{j}*\gamma=0\}

is the space of
Neumann
forms relative to
\partial_{j}\Sigma
. One can check that the restriction of
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma}
to
\mathcal{L}
is nondegenerate. Moreover, one can show (see
[
CMR17
]
for more details) that
\mathcal{L}
is is indeed Lagrangian in the symplectic complement
\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}_{\Sigma}
of
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma}
. One can then construct a
propagator

\mathscr{P}
as the integral of the chain contraction
K
of
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}1}(\Sigma)
onto
H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}1}(\Sigma)
. The gauge-fixing Lagrangian is then given as

\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{im}(K)[1]\oplus\mathrm{im}(K^{*})[d-2].

This can be done by choosing a Hodge chain contraction
K\colon\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{D}1}(\Sigma)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet-1}_{\mathrm{D}1}(\Sigma)
. We call a differential form
\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)

ultra-Dirichlet relative to
\partial_{j}\Sigma
if the pullbacks to
\partial_{j}\Sigma
of all even normal derivatives of
\beta
and pullbacks of all odd normal derivatives of
*\beta
vanish. We call a differential form
\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)

ultra-Neumann relative to
\partial_{j}\Sigma
if pullbacks to
\partial_{j}\Sigma
of all even normal derivatives of
*\beta
and pullbacks of all odd normal derivatives of
\beta
vanish. Denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}j}(\Sigma)
and
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\widehat{\mathrm{N}}j}(\Sigma)
the space of ultra-Dirichlet and ultra-Neumann forms, respectively. We call a differential form
\beta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)

ultra-harmonic
if it is closed with respect to
{\mathrm{d}}
and
{\mathrm{d}}^{*}
. Denote by
\widehat{\mathrm{Harm}}^{\bullet}(\Sigma)
the space of ultra-harmonic forms on
\Sigma
. Note that
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\widehat{\mathrm{D}}j}(\Sigma)
and
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\widehat{\mathrm{N}}j}(\Sigma)
are both subcomplexes of
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)
with respect to
{\mathrm{d}}
and
{\mathrm{d}}^{*}
, respectively. One can easily see that ultra-harmonic Dirichlet forms are ultra-Dirichlet and ultra-harmonic Neumann forms are ultra-Neumann.
Let
\Delta_{\mathrm{Hodge}}:={\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}}^{*}+{\mathrm{d}}^{*}{\mathrm{d}}
denote the Hodge Laplacian.
The Hodge chain contraction is then given by
K={\mathrm{d}}^{*}/(\Delta_{\mathrm{Hodge}}+\pi_{\mathrm{Harm}})
, where
\pi_{\mathrm{Harm}}
denotes the projection to (ultra-)harmonic forms. The propagator
\mathscr{P}
is then a smooth form on the compactified configuration space
\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\Sigma)}:=\overline{\{(u_{1},u_{2})\in\Sigma\times\Sigma\mid u_{1}\not=u_{2}\}}
. Moreover, consider the space
\mathfrak{D}:=\{(u_{1},u_{2})\in(\partial_{1}\Sigma\times\Sigma)\cup(\partial_{2}\Sigma\times\Sigma)\mid u_{1}\not=u_{2}\}
(see Appendix
B
for more on configuration spaces on manifolds with boundary). Then we get that
\mathscr{P}\in\Omega^{d-1}(\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\Sigma)},\mathfrak{D})=\{\gamma\in\Omega^{d-1}(\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\Sigma)})\mid i^{*}_{\mathfrak{D}}\gamma=0\}
, where
i_{\mathfrak{D}}\colon\mathfrak{D}\hookrightarrow\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\Sigma)}
denotes the inclusion. We can write the propagator as a path integral

\mathscr{P}=\frac{1}{T_{\Sigma}}\frac{(-1)^{d}}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}\int_{\mathcal{L}}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma}/\hbar\right)\pi_{1}^{*}\mathscr{X}\pi_{2}^{*}\mathscr{Y},

where
\pi_{j}\colon\Sigma\times\Sigma\rightarrow\Sigma
for
j=1,2
denotes the projection onto the first and second factor, respectively and
T_{\Sigma}=\int_{\mathcal{L}}\exp(\mathrm{i}\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma}/\hbar)\in\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma})/\{\pm 1\}
is given in terms of the Ray–Singer torsion
[
RS71
]
which is defined through zeta regularization which does not depend on the choice of
\mathcal{L}
since the Ray–Singer torsion does not depend on the choice of metric.

Let
N
be a
(d-1)
-manifold and let
\mathcal{H}^{n}_{N,\ell}
be the vector space of
n
-linear functionals on
\Omega^{\bullet}(N)[\ell]
of the form

\Omega^{\bullet}(N)[\ell]\ni\mathds{D}\mapstochar\rightarrow\int_{N^{n}}\gamma\pi^{*}_{1}\mathds{D}\dotsm\pi^{*}_{n}\mathds{D}

up to multiplication with a term given by the Reidemeister torsion
[
Rei35
]
of
N
. We have denoted by
\gamma
a distributional form on
N^{n}
.
The boundary state space
\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
is then given by

\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}=\prod_{n_{1},n_{2}=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{H}^{n_{1}}_{\partial_{1}\Sigma,1}\widehat{\otimes}\mathcal{H}^{n_{2}}_{\partial_{2}\Sigma,d-2}

and
\widehat{\mathcal{H}}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}\widehat{\otimes}\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma})
. Perturbatively, the BV-BFV partition function is asymptotically given by

T_{\Sigma}\exp(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Sigma}/\hbar)\times\\
\times\sum_{j\geq 0}\hbar^{j}\sum_{n_{1},n_{2}\geq 0}\,\int_{(\partial_{1}\Sigma)^{n_{1}}\times(\partial_{2}\Sigma)^{n_{2}}}R^{j}_{n_{1}n_{2}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\pi_{1,1}^{*}\mathds{X}^{i_{1}}\dotsm\pi^{*}_{1,n_{1}}\mathds{X}^{i_{n}}\pi^{*}_{2,1}\mathds{Y}_{i_{1}}\dotsm\pi^{*}_{2,n_{2}}\mathds{Y}_{i_{n}},

where
R^{j}_{n_{1}n_{2}}(\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})
denotes distributional forms on
(\partial_{1}\Sigma)^{n_{1}}\times(\partial_{2}\Sigma)^{n_{2}}
with values in
\mathrm{Dens}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma})
. Note that we sum over
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}
. Moreover, we have denoted by
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Sigma}
the effective action
27
27

27

One can easily check that the partition function
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma,\partial\Sigma}=T_{\Sigma}\exp(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Sigma}/\hbar)
, defined through the effective action
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Sigma}
, satisfies the mQME (
4.4.5
)
given by

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Sigma}=(-1)^{d-1}\left(\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\sum_{i}\mathds{Y}_{i}\mathsf{x}^{i}-\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\sum_{i}\mathsf{y}_{i}\mathds{X}^{i}\right)-(-1)^{2d}\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma\times\partial_{1}\Sigma}\sum_{i}\pi^{*}_{1}\mathds{Y}_{i}\mathscr{P}\pi^{*}_{2}\mathds{X}^{i}.

The BFV boundary operator acting on
\mathcal{H}^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
is then given by the ordered standard quantization of the BFV boundary action
\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}
:

\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}=(-1)^{d}\mathrm{i}\hbar\left(\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\sum_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{Y}_{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{Y}_{i}}+\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\sum_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{X}^{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{X}^{i}}\right).

Suppose now that we have two smooth
d
-manifolds
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
with common boundary component
(d-1)
-manifold
N
. We can then compute the glued partition function
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV}}_{\Sigma}
for the glued manifold
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\cup_{N}\Sigma_{2}
(see Figure
4.5.1
) out of the BV-BFV partition functions
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{1},\partial\Sigma_{1}}
on
\Sigma_{1}
and
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{2},\partial\Sigma_{2}}
on
\Sigma_{2}
. We consider the transversal polarization on
\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{N}
. In particular, we decompose the boundaries
\partial\Sigma_{1}=\partial_{1}\Sigma_{1}\sqcup\partial_{2}\Sigma_{1}
and
\partial\Sigma_{2}=\partial_{1}\Sigma_{2}\sqcup\partial_{2}\Sigma_{2}
such that
N\subset\partial_{1}\Sigma_{1}
and
N^{\mathrm{opp}}\subset\partial_{2}\Sigma_{2}
. Let
\mathds{X}^{N}_{1}
and
\mathds{Y}^{N}_{2}
be the coordinates on
\Omega^{\bullet}(N)[1]
and
\Omega^{\bullet}(N)[d-2]
, respectively. The glued state is then given by

\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV}}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\{\mathds{X}^{N}_{1},\mathds{Y}^{N}_{2}\}}\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(-1)^{d-1}\int_{N}\sum_{i}(\mathds{Y}^{N}_{2})_{i}(\mathds{X}^{N}_{1})^{i}\right)\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{1},\partial\Sigma_{1}}\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{2},\partial\Sigma_{2}}.

The glued partition function can be computed by considering the glued effective action and then considering
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV}}_{\Sigma}=T_{\Sigma_{1}}T_{\Sigma_{2}}\exp(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{eff}}_{\Sigma}/\hbar)
.

Remark 4.6.1
.

This construction can be generalized to the case of
BF
-like theories (perturbations of abelian
BF
theory), see
[
CMR17
]
for more details. In particular, this is important since AKSZ theories often appear as
BF
-like theories.
This will be relevant especially for treating DW theory in the BV-BFV setting by regarding it as an AKSZ theory, which will be the content of Section
5
. An important result regarding the gluing for
BF
-like theories is given by the following proposition.

Proposition 4.6.2

(Cattaneo–Mnev–Reshetikhin
[
CMR17
]
)
.

Let
\Sigma
be cut along a codimension-one submanifold
N
into
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
. Let
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{1},N}
and
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{2},N^{\mathrm{opp}}}
be the boundary states for
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
with a choice of residual fields and propagators and transverse (
\mathds{X}
vs.
\mathds{Y}
) polarizations on
N
. Then the gluing of
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{1},N}
and
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma_{2},N^{\mathrm{opp}}}
is the state
\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma,\partial\Sigma}
for
\Sigma
with the consequent choice of residual fields and propagators.

4.7.
BV-BF
k
V extension and shifted symplectic structures

If we move to higher defects, the symplectic form will be shifted by degree
+1
. In particular, if we consider a codimension
k
submanifold
N_{k}\subset\Sigma
, then the symplectic gauge formalism associated to
N_{k}
will be
(k-1)
-shifted. We will call the theory associated to a codimension
k
submanifold a BF
k
V theory and the coupling for each contiguous codimension (fully extended) a BV-BF
k
V theory associated to the
d
-manifold
\Sigma
. The underlying mathematical theory for shifted symplectic structures was developed first in
[
Pan+13
]
by using methods and the language of derived algebraic geometry and studied further by various people. In
[
Pan+13
]
, they define first a symplectic form on a smooth scheme over some base ring
k
of characteristic zero to be a
2
-form
\omega\in H^{0}(X,\Omega^{2,cl}_{X/k})
which moreover is required to be nondegenerate, i.e. it induces an isomorphism
\Theta_{\omega}
between the tangent and cotangent bundles of
X
.
Then one can define an
n
-shifted symplectic form
on a derived Artin stack
X
(in particular, they consider
X
to be the solution of a
derived moduli problem
(see also
[
Toë14
]
)) to be a closed
2
-form
\omega\in H^{n}(X,\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{L}_{X/k})
of degree
n
on
X
such that the corresponding morphism
\Theta_{\omega}\colon\mathbb{T}_{X/k}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{X/k}[n]
is an isomorphism in the derived category of quasi-coherent sheaves
D^{b}\mathrm{QCoh}(X)
on
X
. Here we have denoted by
\mathbb{L}_{X/k}
the cotangent complex of
X
and by
\mathbb{T}_{X/k}
its dual, the tangent complex of
X
. Of course, it is important to mention what
closed
in this setting actually means. They define closedness of general
p
-forms by interpreting sections of
\bigwedge^{2}\mathbb{L}_{X/k}
as functions on the derived loop stack
\mathcal{L}X
and consider it as some type of
S^{1}
-equivariance property. An important result of
[
Pan+13
]
is an existence result concerning a derived algebraic version of the AKSZ formulation in this setting. In particular, they prove the following theorem:

Theorem 4.7.1

(Pantev–Toën–Vaquié–Vezzosi
[
Pan+13
]
)
.

Let
X
be a derived stack endowed with an
\mathcal{O}
-orientation of dimension
d
, and let
(F,\omega)
be a derived Artin stack with an
n
-shifted symplectic structure
\omega
. Then the derived mapping stack
\mathbf{Map}(X,F)
carries a natural
(n-d)
-shifted symplectic structure.

The BV-BFV formalism, as described in Section
4
, can be easily extended to higher codimensions in the classical setting, but needs more sophisticated methods in the quantum setting
[
CMR14
,
Mos21
]
. As discussed in
[
Mos21
]
, the quantum setting needs to couple the methods of deformation quantization to the methods of geometric quantization, both in the shifted setting. Fortunately, also the methods for shifted Poisson structures and shifted deformation quantization have been developed in
[
Cal+17
,
Saf17
]
. The setting of geometric quantization in the shifted picture has been recently considered in
[
Saf20
]
. The methods developed in
[
Pan+13
]
have been considered for the setting of codimension 1 structures (BV-BFV) in
[
Cal15
]
for the setting of AKSZ topological field theories by using Lagrangian correspondences and have been recently (fully) extended by Calaque, Haugseng and Scheimbauer
[
CHS
]
.

Example 4.7.2

(Chern–Simons theory)
.

As an example we want to discuss the 3-dimensional case of Chern–Simons theory (see Section
3.2
). Recall that the phase space is given by the moduli space of flat
G
connections for some compact Lie group
G
. In the codimension 0 case, we are working over a closed oriented 3-manifold
\Sigma
(see Figure
4.7.1
) which carries the induced BV symplectic structure, i.e. a
(-1)
-shifted structure. In the codimension 1 case we are working over a closed oriented 2-manifold
N_{1}\subset\Sigma
(e.g. some Riemann surface
\Sigma_{g}
of genus
g
), to which we can associate a phase space endowed with the
Atiyah–Bott symplectic structure

[
AB83
]
, which is a 0-shifted structure. In the codimension 2 case we are working over a closed oriented 1-manifold
S^{1}\cong N_{2}\subset\Sigma
, for which the phase space is given by the stack of conjugacy classes
[G/G]
and we can consider a
1
-shifted symplectic structure by the canonical 3-form on
G
. Finally, in the codimension 3 case we consider a point
\mathrm{pt}
(0-manifold), for which the phase space is given by the classifying stack
\mathrm{B}G=[\mathrm{pt}/G]
endowed with the 2-shifted symplectic form given by the invariant pairing (Killing form) on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
.

Figure 4.7.1.
Examples of 2-dimensional defects sitting inside the 3-ball.

5.
AKSZ formulation of DW theory

In this section we want to describe a way of obtaining the DW action functional as in Section
2.3
in terms of an AKSZ theory by choosing a suitable gauge-fixing Lagrangian submanifold
\mathcal{L}

[
BS89
,
Ike11
,
Bon+20
]
.

5.1.
AKSZ data

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a Lie algebra and consider its
Weil model
28
28

28

For a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}
, the Weil model is given by the algebra
\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{h}):=\bigwedge^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{h^{*}}\oplus\mathfrak{h}^{*}[1])
together with a differential
{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{W}}\colon\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{h})\rightarrow\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{h})
such that
(\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{h}),{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{W}})
is a differential graded algebra. The differential is defined to act on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
as the differential for the Chevalley–Eilenberg algebra of
\mathfrak{h}
plus the degree shift differential. In particular
{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{W}}={\mathrm{d}}_{\mathrm{CE}(\mathfrak{h})}+{\mathrm{d}}_{s}
, where
{\mathrm{d}}_{s}
acts by degree shift
\mathfrak{h}^{*}\rightarrow\mathfrak{h}^{*}[1]
for elements in
\mathfrak{h}
and by
0
for elements in
\mathfrak{h}^{*}[1]
.

(\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{h}),{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{W}})
.
Note that
\mathcal{W}(\mathfrak{h})
can be endowed with a natural symplectic form of degree
+3
if
\mathfrak{h}
is endowed with an invariant, nondegenerate symmetric pairing
\langle\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt\rangle
. We want to consider this symplectic manifold to be the target for a 4-dimensional AKSZ theory. The graded vector space
\mathfrak{h}[1]\oplus\mathfrak{h}[2]
is endowed with the symplectic structure

\omega=\langle\delta X,\delta Y\rangle,

where
X
denotes the coordinate of degree
+1
and
Y
denotes the coordinate of degree
+2
. We can define a Hamiltonian of degree
+4
by

\Theta(X,Y)=\frac{1}{2}\langle Y,Y\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle Y,[X,X]\rangle.

One can check that the Hamiltonian vector field of
\Theta
with respect to
\omega
is given by the Weil differential
{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{W}}
. We have

(5.1.1)

{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{W}}X=Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,X],\qquad{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathcal{W}}Y=[X,Y].

Let now
\Sigma
be a 4-manifold and consider the BV space of fields

\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}=\Map(T[1]\Sigma,\mathfrak{h}[1]\oplus\mathfrak{h}[2])\cong\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathfrak{h}[1]\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma)\otimes\mathfrak{h}[2].

The superfields are given by

(5.1.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{X}(u,\theta)

\displaystyle=X+A\theta^{i_{1}}+\chi\theta^{i_{1}}\theta^{i_{2}}+\psi^{\dagger}\theta^{i_{1}}\theta^{i_{2}}\theta^{i_{3}}+Y^{\dagger}\theta^{i_{1}}\theta^{i_{2}}\theta^{i_{3}}\theta^{i_{4}},

(5.1.3)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Y}(u,\theta)

\displaystyle=Y+\psi\theta^{i_{1}}+\chi^{\dagger}\theta^{i_{1}}\theta^{i_{2}}+A^{\dagger}\theta^{i_{1}}\theta^{i_{2}}\theta^{i_{3}}+X^{\dagger}\theta^{i_{1}}\theta^{i_{2}}\theta^{i_{3}}\theta^{i_{4}}.

for local coordinates
(u_{i},\theta^{i})
on
T[1]\Sigma
.
The BV symplectic form is given by

\omega_{\Sigma}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma}\bm{\mu}_{4}\langle\delta\mathbf{X},\delta\mathbf{Y}\rangle,

and the AKSZ-BV action is given by

\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma}\bm{\mu}_{4}\left(\langle\mathbf{Y},{\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{X}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{Y},[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}]\rangle\right).

We have denoted by
\bm{\mu}_{4}:={\mathrm{d}}^{4}u{\mathrm{d}}^{4}\theta
the supermeasure on
T[1]\Sigma
.
If we expand things into components and perform Berezinian integration, we get

(5.1.4)

\omega_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}{\mathrm{d}}^{4}u\left(\delta X\delta X^{\dagger}+\delta A\delta A^{\dagger}+\delta\chi\delta\chi^{\dagger}+\delta\psi^{\dagger}\delta\psi+\delta Y^{\dagger}\delta Y\right)

and

(5.1.5)

\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}{\mathrm{d}}^{4}u\bigg(\langle\psi,{\mathrm{d}}_{A}\chi\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle Y,[\chi,\chi]\rangle+\langle\psi^{\dagger},({\mathrm{d}}_{A}Y+[X,\psi])\rangle\\
+\langle\chi^{\dagger},(F_{A}+[X,\chi])\rangle+\langle A^{\dagger},(\psi+{\mathrm{d}}_{A}X)\rangle+\langle Y^{\dagger},[X,Y]\rangle\\
+\left\langle X^{\dagger},\left(\frac{1}{2}[X,X]\right)\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\chi^{\dagger},\chi^{\dagger}\rangle\bigg),

where
{\mathrm{d}}_{A}={\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}+[A,\kern 5.0pt]
denotes the covariant derivative of
A
and
F_{A}={\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}A+\frac{1}{2}[A,A]
. The BV transformations on the superfields are then given by

(5.1.6)

\displaystyle Q_{\Sigma}\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{Y}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}],

(5.1.7)

\displaystyle Q_{\Sigma}\mathbf{Y}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{Y}+[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}],

i.e., in superfield notation, the cohomological vector field is given by

(5.1.8)

Q_{\Sigma}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma}\bm{\mu}_{4}\Bigg(\left({\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{X}+\mathbf{Y}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}]\right)\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbf{X}}+\big({\mathrm{d}}_{\Sigma}\mathbf{Y}+[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y}]\big)\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbf{Y}}\Bigg)

In component fields and after Berezinian integration, we get the cohomological vector field

(5.1.9)

Q_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}{\mathrm{d}}^{4}u\Bigg(\left(Y+\frac{1}{2}[X,X]\right)\frac{\delta}{\delta X}+(\psi+{\mathrm{d}}_{A}X)\frac{\delta}{\delta A}+(\chi^{\dagger}+F_{A}+[X,\chi])\frac{\delta}{\delta\chi}\\
+({\mathrm{d}}_{A}\chi+A^{\dagger}+[X,\psi^{\dagger}])\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi^{\dagger}}+({\mathrm{d}}_{A}\psi^{\dagger}+X^{\dagger}+[X,Y^{\dagger}])\frac{\delta}{\delta Y^{\dagger}}+[X,Y]\frac{\delta}{\delta Y}\\
+({\mathrm{d}}_{A}Y+[X,\psi])\frac{\delta}{\delta\psi}+({\mathrm{d}}_{A}\psi+[X,\chi^{\dagger}]+[\chi,Y])\frac{\delta}{\delta\chi^{\dagger}}\\
+({\mathrm{d}}_{A}\chi^{\dagger}+[X,A^{\dagger}]+[\psi^{\dagger},Y]+[\chi.\psi])\frac{\delta}{\delta A^{\dagger}}\\
+({\mathrm{d}}_{A}A^{\dagger}+[X,X^{\dagger}]+[Y^{\dagger},Y]+[\psi^{\dagger},\psi]+[\chi,\chi^{\dagger}])\frac{\delta}{\delta X^{\dagger}}\Bigg),

where we have used the relation in (
5.1.1
).

5.2.
A suitable gauge-fixing

Consider a Riemannian metric on
\Sigma
such that we can take a splitting of the fields
\chi,\chi^{\dagger}
into self-dual and anti self-dual parts

(5.2.1)

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle=\chi^{+}+\chi^{-},

(5.2.2)

\displaystyle\chi^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\chi^{+\dagger}+\chi^{-\dagger}.

We can take the gauge-fixing where
\chi^{-}=\chi^{+\dagger}=0
and all other forms are coexact when using Hodge decomposition. This will imply that
X^{\dagger}=Y^{\dagger}=0
. Introduce new fields
\bar{X}
and
b
with
\deg(\bar{X})=\deg(b)=0
and
\gh(\bar{X})=-1
and
\gh(b)=0
together with corresponding anti-fields
\bar{X}^{\dagger}
and
b^{\dagger}
with
\deg(\bar{X}^{\dagger})=\deg(b^{\dagger})=4
, such that
\gh(\bar{X}^{\dagger})=0
and
\gh(b^{\dagger})=-1
, respectively. Moreover, we consider the term

\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}\left(\langle\bar{X}^{\dagger},(b+[X,\bar{X}])\rangle+\langle b^{\dagger},([X,b]-[Y,\bar{X}])\rangle\right).

Additionally, introduce more new fields
(\bar{Y},\eta)
with
\deg(Y)=\deg(\eta)=0
and
\gh(\bar{Y})=-2
and
\gh(\eta)=-1
together with corresponding anti-fields
\bar{Y}^{\dagger}
and
\eta^{\dagger}
with
\deg(\bar{Y}^{\dagger})=\deg(\eta^{\dagger})=4
, such that
\gh(\bar{Y}^{\dagger})=+1
and
\gh(\eta^{\dagger})=0
. We consider the term

\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma}\left(\langle\bar{Y}^{\dagger},(\eta+[X,\bar{Y}]\rangle+\langle\eta^{\dagger},([X,\eta]+[\bar{Y},Y])\rangle\right).

The gauge-fixed BV action is then obtained by

\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{gf}}_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}+\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{\Sigma}+\mathcal{S}^{(2)}_{\Sigma}

with gauge-fixing fermion

(5.2.3)

\varPsi^{\mathrm{gf}}=\int_{\Sigma}\langle\bar{X},{\mathrm{d}}*A\rangle+\int_{\Sigma}\langle\bar{Y},{\mathrm{d}}*\psi\rangle.

such that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{gf}}_{\Sigma}=\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}+Q_{\Sigma}\varPsi^{\mathrm{gf}}
.
The auxiliary fields
\chi^{-\dagger}
have ghost number zero and can be actually integrated out. One can check that the gauge-transformations for each field squares to zero except the one for
\chi^{+}
. Namely, we have

\delta^{2}\chi^{+}=({\mathrm{d}}_{A}\psi)^{+}+[Y,\chi^{+}],

which is the equation of motion for this setting. Hence, the tuple

(A,Y,\psi,\chi^{+},\bar{Y},\eta)

is the same as in the construction of Section
2.3
.

5.3.
BV-BFV formulation

As an AKSZ theory, there a nice way of treating DW theory as a gauge-theory on 4-manifolds with boundary. Let
\Sigma
be a 4-manifold with 3-dimensional boundary
\partial\Sigma
. As obtained in Section
5.1
, the BV theory of the AKSZ construction of DW theory is given by

(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma},\omega_{\Sigma},\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}).

One can then easily formulate a BFV theory on the boundary
\partial\Sigma
by setting

(5.3.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}

\displaystyle:=\Map(T[1]\partial\Sigma,\mathfrak{h}[1]\oplus\mathfrak{h}[2])\cong\Omega^{\bullet}(\partial\Sigma)\otimes\mathfrak{h}[1]\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(\partial\Sigma)\otimes\mathfrak{h}[2],

(5.3.2)

\displaystyle\omega^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}

\displaystyle:=\int_{T[1]\partial\Sigma}\bbmu_{3}\langle\delta\mathds{X},\delta\mathds{Y}\rangle,

(5.3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}

\displaystyle:=\int_{T[1]\partial\Sigma}\bbmu_{3}\left(\langle\mathds{Y},{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{X}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathds{Y},\mathds{Y}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathds{Y},[\mathds{X},\mathds{X}]\rangle\right),

where
\bbmu_{3}:={\mathrm{d}}^{3}\mathbb{u}{\mathrm{d}}^{3}\bbtheta
denotes the supermeasure on
T[1]\partial\Sigma
for local coordinates
(\mathbb{u}_{i},\bbtheta^{i})
on
T[1]\partial\Sigma
.
The boundary superfields are given by

(5.3.4)

\displaystyle\mathds{X}(\mathbb{u},\bbtheta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{X}+\mathbb{A}\bbtheta^{i_{1}}+\bbchi\bbtheta^{i_{1}}\bbtheta^{i_{2}}+\bbpsi^{\dagger}\bbtheta^{i_{1}}\bbtheta^{i_{2}}\bbtheta^{i_{3}}+\mathbb{Y}^{\dagger}\bbtheta^{i_{1}}\bbtheta^{i_{2}}\bbtheta^{i_{3}}\bbtheta^{i_{4}},

(5.3.5)

\displaystyle\mathds{Y}(\mathbb{u},\bbtheta)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{Y}+\bbpsi\bbtheta^{i_{1}}+\bbchi^{\dagger}\bbtheta^{i_{1}}\bbtheta^{i_{2}}+\mathbb{A}^{\dagger}\bbtheta^{i_{1}}\bbtheta^{i_{2}}\bbtheta^{i_{3}}+\mathbb{X}^{\dagger}\bbtheta^{i_{1}}\bbtheta^{i_{2}}\bbtheta^{i_{3}}\bbtheta^{i_{4}}.

The cohomological vector field
Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}
is given by the Hamiltonian vector field of
\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}
:

\iota_{Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}}\omega^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\delta\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}.

It is easy to see that
\omega^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}
is exact with primitive 1-form

\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\int_{T[1]\partial\Sigma}\bbmu_{3}\langle\mathds{Y},\delta\mathds{X}\rangle.

If we expand things into components and perform Berezinian integration, we get

(5.3.6)

\omega^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\int_{\partial\Sigma}{\mathrm{d}}^{3}\mathbb{u}\left(\delta\mathbb{X}\delta\mathbb{X}^{\dagger}+\delta\mathbb{A}\delta\mathbb{A}^{\dagger}+\delta\bbchi\delta\bbchi^{\dagger}+\delta\bbpsi^{\dagger}\delta\bbpsi+\delta\mathbb{Y}^{\dagger}\delta\mathbb{Y}\right)

and

(5.3.7)

\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\int_{\partial\Sigma}{\mathrm{d}}^{3}\mathbb{u}\bigg(\langle\bbpsi,{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\bbchi\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbb{Y},[\bbchi,\bbchi]\rangle+\langle\bbpsi^{\dagger},({\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\mathbb{Y}+[\mathbb{X},\bbpsi])\rangle\\
+\langle\bbchi^{\dagger},(\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{A}}+[\mathbb{X},\bbchi])\rangle+\langle\mathbb{A}^{\dagger},(\bbpsi+{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\mathbb{X})\rangle+\langle\mathbb{Y}^{\dagger},[\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y}]\rangle\\
+\left\langle\mathbb{X}^{\dagger},\left(\frac{1}{2}[\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}]\right)\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\bbchi^{\dagger},\bbchi^{\dagger}\rangle\bigg),

where
{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}={\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}+[\mathbb{A},\kern 5.0pt]
denotes the covariant derivative of
\mathbb{A}
and
\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{A}}={\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathbb{A}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathbb{A},\mathbb{A}]
. The BV transformations on the superfields are then given by

(5.3.8)

\displaystyle Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{X}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{X}+\mathds{Y}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathds{X},\mathds{X}],

(5.3.9)

\displaystyle Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{Y}

\displaystyle={\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{Y}+[\mathds{X},\mathds{Y}],

i.e., in superfield notation, the cohomological vector field is given by

(5.3.10)

Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\int_{T[1]\partial\Sigma}\bbmu_{3}\Bigg(\left({\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{X}+\mathds{Y}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathds{X},\mathds{X}]\right)\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{X}}+\big({\mathrm{d}}_{\partial\Sigma}\mathds{Y}+[\mathds{X},\mathds{Y}]\big)\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{Y}}\Bigg)

In component fields and after Berezinian integration, we get the cohomological vector field

(5.3.11)

Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}=\int_{\partial\Sigma}{\mathrm{d}}^{3}\mathbb{u}\Bigg(\left(\mathbb{Y}+\frac{1}{2}[\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}]\right)\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbb{X}}+(\bbpsi+{\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\mathbb{X})\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbb{A}}+(\bbchi^{\dagger}+\mathbb{F}_{\mathbb{A}}+[\mathbb{X},\bbchi])\frac{\delta}{\delta\bbchi}\\
+({\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\bbchi+\mathbb{A}^{\dagger}+[\mathbb{X},\bbpsi^{\dagger}])\frac{\delta}{\delta\bbpsi^{\dagger}}+({\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\bbpsi^{\dagger}+\mathbb{X}^{\dagger}+[\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y}^{\dagger}])\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbb{Y}^{\dagger}}+[\mathbb{X},\mathbb{Y}]\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbb{Y}}\\
+({\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\mathbb{Y}+[\mathbb{X},\bbpsi])\frac{\delta}{\delta\bbpsi}+({\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\bbpsi+[\mathbb{X},\bbchi^{\dagger}]+[\bbchi,\mathbb{Y}])\frac{\delta}{\delta\bbchi^{\dagger}}\\
+({\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\bbchi^{\dagger}+[\mathbb{X},\mathbb{A}^{\dagger}]+[\bbpsi^{\dagger},\mathbb{Y}]+[\bbchi,\bbpsi])\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbb{A}^{\dagger}}\\
+({\mathrm{d}}_{\mathbb{A}}\mathbb{A}^{\dagger}+[\mathbb{X},\mathbb{X}^{\dagger}]+[\mathbb{Y}^{\dagger},\mathbb{Y}]+[\bbpsi^{\dagger},\bbpsi]+[\bbchi,\bbchi^{\dagger}])\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbb{X}^{\dagger}}\Bigg).

By setting
\pi_{\Sigma}\colon\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}
to be the restriction of the fields to the boundary, we get that the modified CME

Q_{\Sigma}\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}=\pi^{*}_{\Sigma}(2\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}-\iota_{Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}}\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma})

is satisfied and that the cohomological vector field
Q_{\Sigma}
is indeed projectable, i.e. we have

\delta\pi_{\Sigma}Q_{\Sigma}=Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}.

6.
Quantization of DW theory in the BV-BFV setting

6.1.
The BFV boundary operator

Assume now that we can write the boundary as a disjoint union
\partial\Sigma=\partial_{1}\Sigma\sqcup\partial_{2}\Sigma
where
\partial_{1}\Sigma
and
\partial_{2}\Sigma
have opposite orientation. We choose the convenient polarization
\mathcal{P}
on
\partial\Sigma
consisting of choosing the
\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{Y}}
-polarization (
\mathds{X}
-representation) on
\partial_{1}\Sigma
and the
\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{X}}
-polarization (
\mathds{Y}
-representation) on
\partial_{2}\Sigma
(see Figure
4.6.1
).
As we have seen in Section
4.4
, we can compute the BFV boundary operator
\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}
as the ordered standard quantization of the boundary action
\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma}
with respect to the chosen polarization.
We get

\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}=\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}_{0}+\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{pert}},

where

(6.1.1)

\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}_{0}:=\underbrace{\mathrm{i}\hbar\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\sum_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\mathds{X}^{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{X}^{i}}}_{=:\Omega_{0}^{\mathds{X}}}+\underbrace{\mathrm{i}\hbar\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\sum_{i}{\mathrm{d}}_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\mathds{Y}_{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{Y}_{i}}}_{=:\Omega^{\mathds{Y}}_{0}},

and

(6.1.2)

\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathrm{pert}}=\Omega^{\mathds{X}}_{\mathrm{pert}}+\Omega^{\mathds{Y}}_{\mathrm{pert}},

with

(6.1.3)

\Omega^{\mathds{X}}_{\mathrm{pert}}:=\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\left(-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\sum_{i,j}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta\mathds{X}^{i}\delta\mathds{X}^{j}}+\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\sum_{i,j,k}c_{ij}^{k}\mathds{X}^{i}\mathds{X}^{j}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathds{X}^{k}}\right),

and

(6.1.4)

\Omega^{\mathds{Y}}_{\mathrm{pert}}:=\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j}\delta^{i}_{j}\mathds{Y}_{i}\mathds{Y}_{j}-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\sum_{i,j,k}c_{ij}^{k}\mathds{Y}_{k}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta\mathds{Y}_{i}\delta\mathds{Y}_{j}}\right),

where
\delta^{i}_{j}
denotes the Kronecker delta and
c_{ij}^{k}
the structure constants of the Lie bracket
[\kern 5.0pt,\kern 5.0pt]
.

6.2.
Composite fields

In order to regularize the higher functional derivatives, one needs to introduce the concept of
composite fields
as in
[
CMR17
]
. In particular, we want to regard the higher functional derivates as a single first order derivative with respect to the composite field. In order to get it coherent to the naive interpretation where a higher functional derivative concentrates the fields on some diagonal, we should also understand the product of integrals as containing the diagonal contributions for the corresponding composite field. We consider the following bullet product of integrals

(6.2.1)

\left(\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\Phi^{i}\right)\bullet\left(\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\sum_{j}\beta_{j}\Phi^{j}\right):=\\
(-1)^{\mathrm{gh}(\Phi^{i})(\mathrm{gh}(\beta_{j})-3)+3\mathrm{gh}(\alpha_{i})}\left(\int_{\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\partial_{k}\Sigma)}}\sum_{i,j}\pi_{1}^{*}\alpha_{i}\pi_{2}^{*}\beta_{j}\pi^{*}_{1}\Phi^{i}\pi^{*}_{2}\Phi^{j}+\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\sum_{i,j}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}[\Phi^{i}\Phi^{j}]\right),

where
\alpha
and
\beta
are smooth forms which depend on the bulk and residual fields and we denote by
[\Phi^{i}\Phi^{j}]
the composite field. We can now interpret the operator
\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\phi^{ij}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta\Phi^{i}\delta\Phi^{j}}
as
\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\phi^{ij}\frac{\delta}{\delta[\Phi^{i}\Phi^{j}]}
. Hence, we get

\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\phi^{ij}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta\Phi^{i}\delta\Phi^{j}}\left(\left(\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\alpha_{i}\Phi^{i}\right)\bullet\left(\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\beta_{j}\Phi^{j}\right)\right)=\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}\phi^{ij}.

More general, let
I:=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})
be a multi-index and note that we can replace the higher derivative
\frac{\delta^{n}}{\delta\Phi^{i_{1}}\dotsm\delta\Phi^{i_{n}}}
by the first order derivative
\frac{\delta}{\delta[\Phi^{I}]}:=\frac{\delta}{\delta[\Phi^{i_{1}}\dotsm\Phi^{i_{n}}]}
with respect to the composite field
[\Phi^{I}]:=[\Phi^{i_{1}}\dotsm\Phi^{i_{n}}]
. The higher functional derivatives appearing in the BFV boundary operator are then regularized by using composite fields and it acts on the state space given by
regular functionals
, i.e. on the algebra which is generated by linear combinations of expressions of the form

(6.2.2)

\int_{\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{m_{1}}(\partial_{1}\Sigma)}\times\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{m_{2}}(\partial_{2}\Sigma)}}\mathsf{Z}^{J_{1}^{1}\dotsm J_{1}^{\ell_{1}}J_{2}^{1}\dotsm J^{\ell_{2}}\dotsm}_{I_{1}^{1}\dotsm I_{1}^{r_{1}}I_{2}^{1}\dotsm I_{2}^{r_{2}}\dotsm}\pi_{1}^{*}\prod_{j=1}^{r_{1}}\left[\mathds{X}^{I_{1}^{j}}\right]\dotsm\pi_{m_{1}}^{*}\prod_{j=1}^{r_{m_{1}}}\left[\mathds{X}^{I^{j}_{m_{1}}}\right]\times\\
\times\pi^{*}_{1}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{1}}\left[\mathds{Y}_{J^{j}_{1}}\right]\dotsm\pi^{*}_{m_{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{\ell_{m_{2}}}\left[\mathds{Y}_{J^{j}_{m_{2}}}\right],

where
I^{j}_{i}
and
J^{j}_{i}
are multi-indices and
\mathsf{Z}^{J_{1}^{1}\dotsm J_{1}^{\ell_{1}}J_{2}^{1}\dotsm J^{\ell_{2}}\dotsm}_{I_{1}^{1}\dotsm I_{1}^{r_{1}}I_{2}^{1}\dotsm I_{2}^{r_{2}}\dotsm}
is a smooth form on the product
\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{m_{1}}(\partial_{1}\Sigma)}\times\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{m_{2}}(\partial_{2}\Sigma)}
. If we denote by
\exp_{\bullet}
the exponential defined through the bullet product
\bullet
constructed as in (
6.2.1
), we can see that

\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\phi^{I}\frac{\delta}{\delta\Phi^{I}}\Big\langle\exp_{\bullet}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{res}}_{\Sigma}+\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{source}}_{\Sigma}\right)\Big\rangle=\left\langle\int_{\partial_{k}\Sigma}\phi^{I}\frac{\delta}{\delta\Phi^{I}}\exp_{\bullet}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{res}}_{\Sigma}+\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{source}}_{\Sigma}\right)\right\rangle,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{res}}_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\langle\mathsf{y},\mathds{X}\rangle-\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\langle\mathds{Y},\mathsf{x}\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{source}}_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle:=\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\langle\mathscr{Y},\mathds{X}\rangle-\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\langle\mathds{Y},\mathscr{X}\rangle,

The
full boundary state
is then defined through the regularization of composite fields by using (
6.2.2
):

(6.2.3)

\mathsf{Z}^{\bullet,\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma,\partial\Sigma}:=\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma,\partial\Sigma}\Big\langle\exp_{\bullet}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{res}}_{\Sigma}+\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{source}}_{\Sigma}\right)\Big\rangle,

where
\big\langle\kern 5.0pt\big\rangle
denotes the expectation value with respect to
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
, i.e. for an observable
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{F}}
, we have

\big\langle O\big\rangle:=\int\exp(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})/\hbar)O(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})\mathscr{D}[\mathbf{X}]\mathscr{D}[\mathbf{Y}].

Let
I_{1},\ldots,I_{m_{1}}
and
J_{1},\ldots,J_{m_{2}}
be multi-indices for
m_{1},m_{2}\geq 1
.
Denote by
\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}
for
k=1,2
the graphs with
m_{1}
vertices on
\partial_{k}\Sigma
, where vertex
s
has valency
|I_{s}|\geq 1
, with adjacent half-edges oriented inwards and decorated with boundary fields
\left[\Phi^{I_{1}}_{k}\right],\ldots,\left[\Phi^{I_{m_{1}}}_{k}\right]
all evaluated at the point of collapse
\mathbb{u}\in\partial_{k}\Sigma
. We also want them to have
|J_{1}|+\dotsm+|J_{m_{2}}|
outward leaves if
k=1
and
|J_{1}|+\dotsm+|J_{m_{2}}|
inward leaves if
k=2
, decorated with functional derivatives with respect to the boundary fields:

\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\delta}{\delta\left[\Phi^{J_{1}}_{k}\right]},\ldots,\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\delta}{\delta\left[\Phi^{J_{m_{2}}}_{k}\right]}

evaluated at the point of collapse
\mathbb{u}\in\partial_{k}\Sigma
. Moreover, there are no outward leaves if
k=2
and no inward leaves if
k=1
. Denote by
\sigma_{\Gamma_{i}^{\prime}}
the differential form given by

\sigma_{\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}}:=\int_{\overline{\mathrm{C}_{\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{H}^{4})}}\omega_{\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}},

where
\omega_{\Gamma_{k}^{\prime}}
denotes the product of limiting propagators at the point of collapse
\mathbb{u}\in\partial_{k}\Sigma
and vertex tensors.
Then we can construct the corresponding
full BFV boundary operator
through the regularization of composite fields as

(6.2.4)

\Omega^{\bullet,\mathcal{P}}_{\partial\Sigma}=\Omega^{\mathcal{P}}_{0}+\Omega^{\bullet,\mathds{X}}_{\mathrm{pert}}+\Omega^{\bullet,\mathds{Y}}_{\mathrm{pert}},

where

(6.2.5)

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet,\mathds{X}}_{\mathrm{pert}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}\geq 0}\,\sum_{\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}\frac{(-\mathrm{i}\hbar)^{\ell(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime})}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma_{1}^{\prime})|}\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma}\left(\sigma_{\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}}\right)^{J_{1}\dotsm J_{m_{2}}}_{I_{1}\dotsm I_{m_{1}}}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{1}}\left[\mathds{X}^{I_{j}}\right]\left((-1)^{4m_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\hbar)^{m_{2}}\frac{\delta^{|J_{1}|+\dotsm+|J_{m_{2}}|}}{\delta\left[\mathds{X}^{J_{1}}\dotsm\mathds{X}^{J_{m_{2}}}\right]}\right)

(6.2.6)

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet,\mathds{Y}}_{\mathrm{pert}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}\geq 0}\,\sum_{\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}}\frac{(-\mathrm{i}\hbar)^{\ell(\Gamma_{2}^{\prime})}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma_{2}^{\prime})|}\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma}\left(\sigma_{\Gamma_{2}^{\prime}}\right)^{J_{1}\dotsm J_{m_{2}}}_{I_{1}\dotsm I_{m_{1}}}\prod_{j=1}^{m_{1}}\left[\mathds{Y}_{I_{j}}\right]\left((-1)^{4m_{2}}(\mathrm{i}\hbar)^{m_{2}}\frac{\delta^{|J_{1}|+\dotsm+|J_{m_{2}}|}}{\delta\left[\mathds{Y}_{J_{1}}\dotsm\mathds{Y}_{J_{m_{2}}}\right]}\right)

6.3.
Feynman rules

In order to formulate a BV-BFV quantization, i.e. for the boundary state and the BFV boundary operator, we need to consider the Feynman graphs on the source manifold
\Sigma
(see Figure
6.3.1
for an example) for DW theory. The graphs are determined by the Feynman rules of the theory and the corresponding degree count (see Section
6.5
).
The Feynman rules are determined by the AKSZ action functional
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
of DW theory. In particular, the interaction vertices are given by the ones as in Figure
6.3.2
. The boundary vertices are given by Figure
6.3.3
and
6.3.4
.

\mathds{X}
\mathds{X}
\mathds{Y}
\partial_{1}^{(1)}\Sigma
\partial_{1}^{(2)}\Sigma
\partial_{2}\Sigma

Example of a Feynman graph on the source manifold.

Figure 6.3.2.
The Feynman rules for DW theory. The first type of vertex corresponds to the term
\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{Y},\mathbf{Y}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\mathbf{Y}_{i}\mathbf{Y}_{i}
. There are two outgoing arrows and no incoming arrow for the first vertex type. The second type of vertex corresponds to the term
\frac{1}{2}\langle\mathbf{Y},[\mathbf{X},\mathbf{X}]\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j,k}c^{k}_{ij}\mathbf{X}^{i}\mathbf{X}^{j}\mathbf{Y}_{k}
, where
c^{k}_{ij}
are the structure constants of the Lie bracket and where we have denoted
c^{k}(\mathbf{X}):=c^{k}_{ij}\mathbf{X}^{i}\mathbf{X}^{j}
. There are at most two incoming arrows and exactly one outgoing arrow for the second vertex type. Finally, we also have leaves corresponding to the residual fields. Note that an arrow between vertices represents the propagator from the starting to the end point.

The Feynman rules for composite field vertices on the boundary

6.4.
The partition function

The partition function (or boundary state) is then formally given by the functional integral where we integrate out the fluctuations, i.e. by

(6.4.1)

\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma,\partial\Sigma}(\mathds{X},\mathds{Y},\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y};\hbar)=\int_{\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{Y}^{\prime}}\exp\left(\mathrm{i}\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{X},\mathbf{Y})/\hbar\right)\mathscr{D}[\mathscr{X}]\mathscr{D}[\mathscr{Y}]

where the Lagrangian submanifold is given by the gauge-fixing as in Section
5.2
, i.e.

\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{graph}({\mathrm{d}}\varPsi^{\mathrm{gf}}),

where
\varPsi^{\mathrm{gf}}
is the gauge-fixing fermion constructed as in (
5.2.3
). The integral (
6.4.1
) is defined perturbatively as a formal power series in
\hbar
in terms of the Feynman graphs given by the according Feynman rules as in Section
6.3
, i.e. we can rewrite it as a perturbative expansion around critical points of
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma}
as

(6.4.2)

\mathsf{Z}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{BV-BFV}}_{\Sigma,\partial\Sigma}(\mathds{X},\mathds{Y},\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y};\hbar)=T_{\Sigma}\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\sum_{\Gamma}\frac{(-\mathrm{i}\hbar)^{\ell(\Gamma)}}{|\mathrm{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\int_{\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{\Gamma}(\Sigma)}}\omega_{\Gamma}(\mathds{X},\mathds{Y},\mathsf{x},\mathsf{y})\right),

where we sum over connected Feynman graphs
\Gamma
and where
\ell(\Gamma)
denotes the number of loops of
\Gamma
. Moreover, the integral in (
6.4.2
) is over the configuration space of the vertex set of
\Gamma
regarded as points in
\Sigma
where we integrate a differential form
\omega_{\Gamma}
depending on the boundary fields and residual fields.

6.5.
Degree count and Feynman graphs

Note that we want the dimension of the configuration space
\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{\Gamma}(\Sigma)}
to match the form degree of the differential form
\omega_{\Gamma}
in order to perform the integrals in (
6.4.2
). Note that the dimension of the configuration space is given by

\dim\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{\Gamma}(\Sigma)}=4n+3m,

where
n
denotes the amount of vertices in the bulk and
m
the amount of vertices on the boundary. Using the fact that the propagator
\mathscr{P}\in\Omega^{3}(\overline{\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\Sigma)})
is a 3-form on the configuration space of two points, the form degree of the differential form
\omega_{\Gamma}
is given by
6\cdot\#I+3\cdot\#II
, where
\#I
denotes the amount of first type vertices and
\#II
the amount of second type vertices as in Figure
6.3.2
. Thus we get the system of equations

(6.5.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}4n+3m&=6\cdot\#I+3\cdot\#II,\\
n&=\#I+\#II.\end{split}

Now if
m=0
, we get
2\cdot\#I=\#II
. Hence, one can check that the diagrams which give a contribution are either wheels with an even amount of type
II
vertices and
\frac{1}{2}\cdot\#I
vertices attached to them as in Figure
6.5.1
.
